### **@artemiosarthur** | 👍 120 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZPrecJHUOUs&lc=UgwnJGAyQnWXOtvXKch4AaABAg)

Professor Jiang, thank you for the insightful discussion on Freud and the Frankfurt School. One point I felt was underrepresented is Carl Jung’s position. Jung was mentioned only briefly, yet his work fundamentally conflicted with Freud’s reduction of psychology to sexual desire.

Jung argued that the psyche cannot be explained solely through libido and repression, but must also include archetypes, the collective unconscious, and a symbolic dimension of meaning-something closer to what many traditions describe as a “divine spark” or inner source of transcendence.

In this sense, Jung did not simply diverge from Freud technically, but challenged the very ideological foundation of Freudian materialism. Including this contrast more explicitly could add important depth to the discussion of modern psychological thought and its philosophical implications.

I believe you're wrong about Carl Jung. Jung fundamentally deviated from Freud because their core philosophies were incompatible. Unlike Freud, Jung delved deeply into the study of the Gnostic Christ, alchemy, and mysticism. He posited the existence of a 'Collective Unconscious,' which operates similarly to the Gnostic concept of the Monad or Pleroma. Furthermore, Jung argued that the Ego creates an illusion of separation, blinding us to the Self—the archetype of wholeness that serves as the psychological equivalent of the 'divine spark' within.

### **@solinvictus74** | 👍 17 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZPrecJHUOUs&lc=Ugx3fsmCOiPowlt99St4AaABAg)

1:25:39
Excellent video as always. I just want to clarify something because I think it's necessary.

Carl Gustav Jung began as a "disciple" of Freud, only to later completely distance himself from his ideas and develop his own psychological analysis that doesn't exclusively focus on the sexual sphere (as Freud did).

In fact, for Freud, the concept of "libido" concerns exclusively the sexual sphere, while for Jung the same concept becomes "psychic energy."

To say that Jung is a disciple of Freud is a grave error; they are two completely different approaches to psychology. Jung himself will have repercussions early in his career due to his choice to distance himself from Freud.

The concept of the Monad, for example, is intrinsic to Jungian psychology, especially with the concept of the anima-animus and the influence the unconscious has on our daily lives.

I hope this comment will be useful to everyone.

I've really enjoyed this whole lecture series, but this one has been pretty off when it comes to Marx, either by misinterpreting or making leaps to connect his broader points. 

1. Marx did not advocate for a Vanguard party, Lenin did! 
Bakunin did criticise Marx for how his Scientific Socialism was creating an intellectual elite. And perhaps Lenin used that to justify his vanguard party. But Marx was very anti-state,  but pro-workers state through federalism/ workers council democracy.


2. Marx was critical of Liberalism because it was the ideology of Capitalism, not for the freedom of the people, but freedom for private property! 

Saw communism as a way for the individual to develop IN THE COMMUNITY. “the free development of each is the condition for the free development of all.”

So the leap to him being a Frankist is a leap and not a thorough enough examination of Marx.

3. The book by Richard Spence has been critqued at again for making huge leaps and bordering on being conspiratorial. 
I see the Transnational Capitalist wanting to collapse Russia and can see the alliance of the White army being one not of ideological alliance but wanting to get their owed debts back. But it's hard to see the USSR as being part of an empire because it's been in opposition to western empire. 
Also, the USSR could have gone and joined the Germans in their revolution if Britain wanted to use the USSR to collapse Germany.

The idea that Marxism is part of the transnational Empire control mechanism doesn't hold up when Western society is very anti-Marx. Think the Red Scare and how Republicans always talk about "Marxist radicals taking over!'

### **@gregwarrener4848** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZPrecJHUOUs&lc=UgzIxiA5DI7TPS2tL9x4AaABAg)

Iron Law of Oligarchy (Robert Michels)

All complex organizations, regardless of how democratic or mission-driven they start, tend to evolve into self-preserving elites.

In your A → B framing:

Class A = mission-driven, corrective, finite-purpose organization

Class B = self-preserving, bureaucratic, power-maintaining organism

Michels’ claim wasn’t moral — it was structural:

Expertise concentrates

Decision-making centralizes

Leaders prioritize continuity

The organization’s survival becomes the implicit goal

Closely related laws (often confused together)

You were also implicitly invoking a cluster of effects:

Goodhart’s Law
“When a measure becomes a target, it ceases to be a good measure.”
→ Metrics replace meaning.

Parkinson’s Law
“Work expands to fill the time available for its completion.”
→ Bureaucracy expands regardless of need.

Mission Drift
→ Original purpose becomes symbolic while incentives shift.

Institutional Capture
→ Regulated systems align with the interests of those they regulate.

### **@watkinsbrown** | 👍 5 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZPrecJHUOUs&lc=UgwhhwetU7xnrNpMH0B4AaABAg)

It's false that Marx was in favor of dictatorship. "Dictatorship of the proletariat" was a humorous figure of speech. To read his writing is to understand he has a strong sense of sarcasm and humor. Marx was an advocate for democracy and laid out in extreme detail why socialism would be the most democratic structure for society.

I have listened to every lecture in this series/semester in its entirety and there were several times when I knew what prof Xiang said to be untrue. Like he says frequently throughout the course, he himself is in a process of learning. This man is a fountain of deep knowledge and he's probably read more on any given subject than most of us watching have read in total in our entire lives. That's why I let it go, because the complete picture is the thing, not small details.

This wide ranging knowledge consumption leads occasionally to blind spots. I believe he is further along the right track than any teacher (or person) I ever encountered, and Im with him in this process, but nobody is flawless. Ive learned easily as much from his lectures as I did from my college education with a philosophy minor. The comments on the vanguard in this lecture and other criticisms of Marx from past lectures are valid, it's just this one thing I can't let go.

I would even go as far as to say Marx had the divine spark, and the way it came out was imperfect due to the people around him (as discussed in the video), his specific education and how it shaped him, the time in history when he lived...but he was definitely onto something, very smart and a good person.

The major problem for peoples, groups, or cultures seeking global hegemony is the distribution of the human species across the planet, given that one can travel on foot across three continents: Africa, Europe, and Asia, with the exception of the American continent, which requires the use of maritime transport, though not necessarily sophisticated if one passes through Greenland or the Bering Strait. But England is already an island, necessarily inhabited by people who undoubtedly possess experience and means of maritime navigation—circumnavigation, of course. It's worth remembering that humans don't emerge from underground like plants, so wherever we find them, we can be sure they came from elsewhere. The Angles, Jutes, and Saxons, forming the colonial population of England, are believed to have originated in Germany and Denmark. It really seems as though, upon colonizing an island after leaving a continent, these people, surrounded by a sea untouched by humankind, thought: we will take all this as we took this island, because apparently it belongs to no one. But a continental, non-insular country had preceded them: Spain. The philosophy of the Spanish Catholic Church in the 15th century, after the Reconquista, was that the world should belong to Christians, according to the "Doctrine of Discovery." Therefore, if people wanted to keep their property, they had to convert to Christianity, and "people" refers to the other non-Christian peoples of the Earth. But Spain (along with its Portuguese neighbor, with a link between the two monarchies, a certain Christopher Columbus or Cristóbal Colón hence colonialism) had shown the way to other European countries, which would not only imitate it but also, over time, oust it from almost all of its colonies. This European wave of Colonization, pillage, and spoliation, and sometimes attempted genocide and democide to alter the demographics of the "discovered" territories, paradoxically constitute the current state of affairs regarding the Christianization of the world by Europe, not by the Companions of Jesus (Peace be upon Him), and as we see, with methods that He and His Companions would certainly have condemned. It looks good in the statistics, alongside the subsequent de-Christianization of the European masses. European colonialism used its mastery of maritime navigation design against peoples who had not developed their navies because these cultures were attached to their territories and had no expansionist ambitions. These were also ancient cultures that had experienced their golden age without inventing a Renaissance myth. And ultimately, globalists would have used the European colonial empires, which, once their task was accomplished, had to pack up and return home. We are in a state of globalization and international division of labor, the north with 1000 factories, the south with 1000 quarries, and the eminently human injustice that prohibits any equitable sharing.

Excellent lecture as always, but there's one point I'd like to make. Carl Jung actually distanced himself and "broke" with Freud's psychoanalytic approach due to the excessive focus on sexuality. Jung's Analytical Psychology focuses more on how symbols are representations of archetypes that all people, from all cultures and ethnicities, have in common. He also presents similar ideas about reflection, meditation, and how to find inner peace in what he calls "individuation", which is integrating all parts of your personality and expanding your consciousness, embracing the Self. Which is also called Monad, The Light, and other names.

I’d argue the reason natural selection became popular so quickly is that it made sense of the growing evidence pre Darwin. These include Linnaean taxonomy, geologic time and heritability and variation of traits that had already been observed in selective breeding. Darwin made the perfectly logical step that nature selects for heritable traits just as a farmer does. The animals Darwin observed, and the Fossil record seemed to reflect this fact and we have some very intricate fossils of animal lineage that follow this order quite plainly. The concept of “favoured races” was also attractive to 19th century imperialists for obvious reasons. Darwin was actually against evolution being used as an argument for slavery and subjugation.

### **@obinowankenobi**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZPrecJHUOUs&lc=UgwgJFbhIeh3RIU2RX14AaABAg)

1:04:43 Marx did believe in (statist) democracy. His ideal form of government was what happened in the Paris Commune, which was basically a direct democracy. The "Dictatorship of the Proletariat" was using the term dictatorship according to the old Roman definition of what we would call a state of emergency. The idea was that the revolution would take control of the state and then institute a state of emergency in order to crush any counter-revolution, and since the economy would be democratized, the state would eventually simply lose all purpose and cease to exist (since again in Marx's view, the mode of production determines the political superstructure). It's a stupid idea but that's what he thought, and Mikhail Bakunin and other anarchists pointed out that what would actually happen is that you'd just get a new bureaucratic ruling class instead, but instead of listening, Marx kicked the anarchists out of the first international.

The debate between Marx and Bakunin should really be seen as a debate between centralization (Marx) and Federalism (Bakunin). Marx wanted a strong state to crush the enemies of the revolution, Bakunin wanted more checks and balances to keep power in the hands of the people. Then after both of them were dead, Lenin came in and just intensified the concentration of power in the state, all to "protect the revolution", especially from itself.

"When the people are being beaten with a stick, they aren't much happier if it is called 'the People's stick.'"

Now that I finished with the popcorn 🍿, let's do some research:

## High Accuracy Claims (7-10/10)

### 🟢 Engels Funding Marx — **10/10**

- 🟢 **Friedrich Engels** came from a **wealthy textile manufacturing family**
- 🟢 Over **20 years**, he sent **£3,000-4,000** to Marx in London
- 🟢 **Completely factually accurate** and well-documented

### 🟢 Russian 1917 Election Results — **10/10**

- 🟢 **Socialist Revolutionaries** won approximately **37-58%** of votes
- 🟢 **Bolsheviks** received about **22-25%** (~9-10 million of ~42 million votes)
- 🟢 These were **Russia's first free elections**

### 🟢 Ottoman Empire Debt — **9/10**

- 🟢 **Substantially true** — Empire took first **foreign loans in 1854-1855**
- 🟢 By **1875**, **two-thirds of state revenue** went to **debt repayment**
- 🟢 The **1881 Muharrem Decree** established European-controlled debt administration

### 🟡 Disraeli's "Coningsby" Content — **8/10**

- 🟡 **Largely accurate** — novel features character **Sidonia** (based on **Lionel de Rothschild**)
- 🟡 Character expounds theories of **Jewish intellectual and racial superiority**
- 🟡 Historians note it inadvertently fueled **anti-Semitic conspiracy theories**

### 🟡 Frankist Antinomian Practices — **8/10**

- 🟡 **Historically documented** — **Frankism** was a real **Sabbatean movement**
- 🟡 **Gershom Scholem's** scholarly research documents **antinomianism** (deliberate violation of religious law)
- 🟡 The **1756 Lanškroun incident** is documented historical evidence

### 🟡 Freud's Seduction Theory Reversal — **8/10**

- 🟡 **Substantially accurate** — Freud developed "**seduction theory**" in 1890s
- 🟡 Around **1897**, Freud **abandoned this theory**, claiming patients had **fantasized abuse**
- 🟡 **Feminist scholars** like **Florence Rush** and **Jeffrey Masson** criticized this reversal

### 🟡 Benjamin Disraeli as PM — **7/10**

- 🟡 **Technically true but misleading** — born Jewish, **baptized Anglican at age 12**
- 🟡 **Jews couldn't serve in Parliament until 1858**
- 🟡 He was **legally and religiously Christian** throughout his political life

### 🟡 Darwin's Rapid Acceptance — **7/10**

- 🟡 **Substantially accurate** — "**On the Origin of Species**" was an **immediate bestseller**
- 🟡 First edition **sold out on publication day** (November 24, 1859)
- 🟡 The "**limited evidence**" claim is **ideological critique**, not historical fact

### 🟡 Heartland Geopolitical Theory — **7/10**

- 🟡 **Accurate regarding geopolitical theory** — **Halford Mackinder's** "**Heartland Theory**" (1904)
- 🟡 Did influence **British foreign policy thinking**
- 🟡 However, transcript **exaggerates** this into a **grand conspiracy narrative**

---

## Moderate Accuracy Claims (4-6/10)

### 🟡 Foreign Volunteers in Red Army — **6/10**

- 🟡 **Partially true** — **Chinese workers**, **Red Latvian Riflemen**, **Hungarian communists** did serve
- 🔴 Calling them "**mercenaries**" is **misleading** — most were **ideological volunteers**
- 🔴 The "**Wall Street funding**" connection is **unsubstantiated**

### 🟡 Jesuits and Conversos — **5/10**

- 🟡 **Partially true** — some early Jesuits had **converso** (Jewish convert) **ancestry**
- 🟡 **Ignatius of Loyola** may have had converso ancestry and **welcomed conversos**
- 🔴 The claim "**first Jesuits were Jews**" **overstates** the evidence

### 🔴 Dönme Participation in Young Turks — **4/10**

- 🟡 **Dönme** did exist as a **real historical group** from **17th-century Izmir**
- 🔴 Some Dönme participated, but movement was **NOT "controlled"** by them
- 🔴 This claim is a **longstanding conspiracy theory** in Turkish politics

---

## Low Accuracy Claims (1-3/10)

### 🔴 Rothschild Waterloo Conspiracy — **3/10**

- 🔴 This is a **conspiracy myth** — story is **heavily distorted**
- 🔴 Rothschild did **NOT crash the market** through **false rumors**
- 🟡 His fortune came from **legitimate bond trading**, not market manipulation

### 🔴 Wall Street Funded Bolsheviks — **3/10**

- 🔴 **Not mainstream history** — claim comes from **Antony Sutton's 1974 book**
- 🔴 **Mainstream historians find insufficient evidence** for systematic funding
- 🟡 **Grains of truth** about individual financiers, but **systematic funding claim unproven**

### 🔴 British Funded Young Turks — **1/10**

- 🔴 **Unverified and likely false** — no evidence found
- 🔴 Young Turks emerged from **internal Ottoman dissatisfaction**
- 🔴 **Unsubstantiated conspiracy claim**

### 🔴 Bank of England as Ponzi Scheme — **1/10**

- 🔴 **False — political rhetoric**, not factual
- 🔴 Government bonds are backed by **taxation authority** and **economic productivity**
- 🔴 This is **propaganda**, not historical fact

### **@lollypawps3396**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZPrecJHUOUs&lc=UgzTAeIClcepJ-6Kk0Z4AaABAg)

Hey Jiang, I love your videos, but to brush over Carl Jung as simply a disciple of Sigmund Freud is a wild oversimplification. Carl Jung and Sigmund Freud together laid the foundation of Psychology and Neuroscience, yes, however they had a huge falling out over Freud's emphasis on 'the Oedipus complex' and 'the repression of infantile sexual fantasies of the parents'. Freud effectively said 'at the root of everyone is sexual repression' while Jung heavily refuted that, ultimately concluding that there was a 'divine spark' within all of us and, above that, that Archetypes aka (gods) speak through us, which is exactly what you have said in the past with your analysis of the Iliad etc. If you read Jung's later works, you would find that he strongly dislikes Freud, and that his work is inspired and virtuous. 
I would also question your conclusion after this, that looking inwards 'gets you nowhere'. Looking inwards is important for self reflection, for understanding yourself, watching your thoughts, and, if you look deep enough through meditation etc, you can feel/see that divine spark within the void of nothingness. Jesus himself says the 'kingdom of heaven is within you' so why would looking inwards 'get you nowhere'?

1:03:44 no he didn't. Marx believed in a mass movement driven by class consciousness. It was Lenin that created the idea of a vanguard party being necessary, and he was condemned by a lot of his contemporary marxists as a closet blanquist who was co-opting the movement because of it (and the criticism is pretty accurate).

Edit: your idea that Marx was being influenced by Engels is definitely correct to a certain extent. Marx was definitely more anti-authoritarian than Engels was, not as much as Bakunin, but it is very notable that the essay that all Marxists like to cite when debating Anarchists is Engels's "On Authority", a piece of shit essay where he literally equates all force, including the force of inanimate objects and gravity, with authority and literally says that a slave that uses violence against his slave master is being authoritarian. If everything is authoritarian then nothing is and your only real choice is who you bow down to. That's his actual argument. Marxists continue to think that this is a good argument for some inexplicable reason despite the fact that anyone who thinks about it for a few seconds can see that it has more holes in it than Swiss cheese.

>   
> "Predictive History" is propaganda-optimized branding: memorable, intellectually flattering, and structurally designed to make interpretive, ideological content feel like neutral, scientific foresight. It's not overtly manipulative on the surface, which makes it all the more effective.
